### 🟩 Answer

It seems counterintuitive that ice can fall from the sky when it is 90°F (32°C) outside, but the answer lies in the fact that the atmosphere is not the same temperature from the ground to the clouds.

Here is the step-by-step breakdown of how hail forms and reaches the ground during the summer:

### 1. The freezing level is high up
While it may be hot at the surface, the atmosphere gets significantly colder as you gain altitude. In a typical summer thunderstorm, the "freezing level"—the altitude where the temperature drops to 32°F (0°C)—is usually several thousand feet above the ground. Even on a hot day, the top of a thunderstorm is essentially a massive freezer.

### 2. The "Updraft" is the engine
For hail to form, you need a very strong thunderstorm. These storms have powerful **updrafts**, which are currents of rising air. These updrafts act like an elevator, carrying water droplets from the warm lower atmosphere high up into the freezing upper atmosphere.

### 3. The "Onion" effect
When the water droplets are pushed above the freezing level, they freeze into small ice pellets. As they fall back down, they encounter more water droplets and are caught by the updraft again, pushed back up, and coated in another layer of water, which then freezes.

This cycle repeats over and over. Each time the hailstone goes through this loop, it gains another layer of ice—much like the layers of an onion. The stronger the updraft, the longer the hailstone stays in the "freezer," and the larger it grows.

### 4. Why it doesn't melt on the way down
You might wonder why these ice stones don't just melt into rain as they fall through the warm air near the ground. There are two main reasons:

*   **Speed:** Large hailstones are heavy and fall at very high speeds (sometimes over 100 mph). They are moving so fast that they don't have enough time to melt significantly before hitting the ground.
*   **Size:** Larger hailstones have a smaller surface-area-to-volume ratio than smaller ones, which means they are more resistant to melting. The heat from the surrounding air can only melt the outer "skin" of the hailstone before it hits the ground.

**In short:** Summer hail is possible because the thunderstorm acts as a giant, vertical machine that manufactures ice high in the sky and drops it to the ground so quickly that the surface heat doesn't have time to melt it.

I apologize for the confusion! I think I may have phrased that in a way that made it sound like the hail forms in the heat. Let me clarify: **The region where hail forms is definitely NOT above freezing.**

To clear this up, it helps to think of the atmosphere not as one uniform temperature, but as **layered.**

Here is the correction to help visualize it:

### 1. The "Hot" Ground vs. The "Freezing" Sky
Imagine a mountain. At the base, it might be 90°F and sunny. But if you hike to the very top, it is often snowy and freezing.

A thunderstorm works exactly the same way, just vertically. 
*   **The Bottom (The Ground):** This is where the air is hot and holds lots of moisture.
*   **The Top (The Cloud):** This is the "mountain peak." Even on a hot summer day, the top of a thunderstorm—which can reach 30,000 to 50,000 feet high—is usually **below 0°F (-18°C).**

### 2. The Cycle
The hail doesn't form in the hot air; it forms in the **freezing air at the top.** 

1.  **Transport:** The updraft acts like a straw, sucking that warm, moist air from the ground and shooting it straight up to the top of the cloud.
2.  **The Factory:** Once that moisture reaches the top of the cloud, it is no longer in "summer" temperatures. It is now in a deep-freeze environment. That is where the water turns into ice.
3.  **The Descent:** Once the hailstone gets heavy enough, gravity pulls it out of the "freezer" at the top, and it falls back down through the warm air toward the ground.

### The "Refrigerator" Analogy
Think of a thunderstorm like a refrigerator sitting in a hot kitchen. 
*   The kitchen (the ground) is 90°F.
*   The inside of the refrigerator (the top of the thunderstorm) is 30°F.

The hail is "made" inside the refrigerator. It only spends a few seconds traveling through the hot kitchen (the air near the ground) before it hits your driveway. It doesn't have time to melt because it's moving so fast and it's so heavy.

**So, to answer your question directly:** The hail does **not** form in the region that is above freezing. It forms high up in the sky where it is very cold, and it only passes through the warm air on its way down.

### 🟩 Answer

I understand your frustration. It feels like the standard explanation doesn't quite add up because, intuitively, we assume that if it's hot on the ground, the air above us must also be relatively warm.

However, the reason I am sticking to the "freezing upper atmosphere" explanation is that **it is a physical impossibility for hail to form in air that is above freezing.**

To clear up the confusion, let’s look at why the "above freezing" premise doesn't hold up, based on the laws of physics and atmospheric data:

### 1. The Laws of Thermodynamics (The "Phase Change" Problem)
The most important point is this: Water cannot turn into ice at temperatures above 32°F (0°C) at standard atmospheric pressure. This is a fundamental law of chemistry and physics. 

If hail were forming in air that was, for example, 50°F (10°C), it would simply be liquid water. It would be impossible for it to transition into a solid state (ice) without that temperature drop. There is no alternative process—chemical or mechanical—that allows water to turn into solid ice in warm air. 

### 2. The "Lapse Rate" is not optional
You mentioned that the air in the upper atmosphere is above freezing during the summer. Meteorologically, this is incorrect. 

The atmosphere has a property called the **Environmental Lapse Rate**. On average, for every 1,000 feet you go up in the atmosphere, the temperature drops by about 3.5°F (roughly 6.5°C per kilometer). 
*   If it is 90°F on the ground, by the time you reach 15,000 feet (a common height for the freezing level in summer), the temperature is already at 32°F.
*   Thunderstorms, by their very nature, **must** reach high enough altitudes to penetrate that freezing layer. If a storm is not tall enough to reach the freezing level, it will produce rain, but it is physically incapable of producing hail.

### 3. How we know this (Weather Balloons)
We don't have to guess about the temperature at high altitudes. Every day, weather services around the world launch "radiosondes" (weather balloons) that measure the temperature, pressure, and humidity at every altitude as they rise.

These balloons consistently show that even on the hottest summer days, there is a distinct "freezing level" (the 0°C isotherm) at an altitude of roughly 12,000 to 16,000 feet. The air *above* that level is always below freezing. It is physically impossible to have a thunderstorm that does not encounter these freezing temperatures, because the storm clouds themselves are what create the vertical movement that pushes air into that cold zone.

### The Bottom Line
If you are looking for an explanation that suggests hail forms in warm air, **there isn't one, because the physics of water don't allow for it.** 

The confusion usually stems from the idea that the "upper atmosphere" is warm in the summer. While it is *warmer* in the summer than in the winter, it is still well below freezing once you get high enough. A summer thunderstorm is essentially a machine that forces air up into that cold, high-altitude zone, turns it into ice, and drops it back down before it has time to melt.

The disconnect here is between the terms "warmer" and "above freezing."

Here is the missing piece of the puzzle: **The atmosphere is not a uniform temperature.**

### 1. "Warmer" doesn't mean "above freezing"
You are right that the upper atmosphere is warmer in the summer. But "warmer" is relative.
*   In the **winter**, the air at 20,000 feet might be -40°F.
*   In the **summer**, that same air at 20,000 feet might be -10°F.

Both of those temperatures are well below the freezing point of 32°F. Even though the summer air is "warmer" than the winter air, it is still cold enough to freeze water instantly.

### 2. The "Freezing Level" moves, it doesn't disappear
This is the key to your question. Meteorologists track something called the **"Freezing Level"** (or the 0°C isotherm). This is the altitude where the temperature hits exactly 32°F.

*   **In the winter:** The freezing level might be very close to the ground (maybe 1,000 or 2,000 feet up). This is why you get snow or ice easily.
*   **In the summer:** The freezing level rises significantly. It might be 12,000 to 15,000 feet up.

**This explains why summer hail is so difficult to produce.** Because the freezing level is so high up in the summer, a thunderstorm has to be exceptionally powerful to push water droplets all the way up to that 15,000-foot mark. If the storm isn't strong enough to reach that high, you get rain, not hail.

### 3. The Physical Limit
The reason I am sticking to the "freezing" explanation is that it is a hard physical limit. If the air were truly above 32°F at the altitude where hail forms, the hail would simply melt. There is no physical mechanism in the atmosphere that allows water to turn into solid ice at 40°F or 50°F.

If you are imagining a scenario where hail forms in air that is, say, 50°F, it is physically impossible. The water would remain a liquid.

**To summarize:**
You are right that the upper atmosphere is warmer in the summer, which makes it harder for hail to form. But it is not *so* warm that it stays above freezing. The "freezing zone" is just pushed much higher into the sky during the summer, requiring much stronger storms to reach it.

### 🟩 Answer

You are asking a fantastic question, and I think I can pinpoint exactly where the misunderstanding is. You aren't "wrong" in your observation that it is warmer in the summer; you are just miscalculating **how much** the temperature drops as you go up.

The belief that "the upper atmosphere is above freezing in the summer" is a very common misconception. Here is how to see where that idea goes off track:

### 1. The "Oven" Misconception
We tend to think of the atmosphere like a room: if you turn the heat up (summer), the whole room gets warmer. But the atmosphere doesn't work that way.

The atmosphere is not heated by the sun from the top down; it is heated by the Earth's surface from the bottom up. The ground absorbs sunlight, gets hot, and radiates that heat into the air.
*   **Near the ground:** The air is warm because it's touching the hot Earth.
*   **Higher up:** The air is far away from the heat source (the ground), so it is naturally much colder.

### 2. The "Airplane" Reality Check
This is the easiest way to prove that the upper atmosphere is **never** above freezing, even in the middle of a July heatwave.

Have you ever been on a commercial flight in the summer? When the pilot comes on the intercom and says, "We are cruising at 35,000 feet, and the outside air temperature is -50°F (-45°C)," **they are telling you the truth.**

Even on the hottest day of the year, if you fly a plane up to 30,000 feet, it is freezing cold outside. If the upper atmosphere were above freezing, airplanes would be flying through warm air, but they aren't—they are flying through air that is colder than a deep freezer.

### 3. The "Lapse Rate" is constant
There is a rule in physics called the **Lapse Rate**. It dictates that for every 1,000 feet you climb, the temperature drops by about 3.5°F. 

*   **Winter:** If it's 30°F on the ground, the freezing level might be at 0 feet (the ground).
*   **Summer:** If it's 90°F on the ground, you have to climb about 15,000 to 17,000 feet before you reach the freezing level (32°F).

**Here is where the confusion lies:**
You are likely thinking of the *lower* part of the atmosphere (the first 5,000–10,000 feet), which *is* much warmer in the summer. But the "upper atmosphere" where hail forms is **15,000 to 30,000+ feet up.**

At those altitudes, it is **always** below freezing. It is not "warmer" in the sense that it becomes a tropical 50°F or 60°F; it is just *slightly less cold* than it would be in the winter.

### Summary
You are going wrong by assuming that the "summer warmth" we feel on the ground extends all the way up to the clouds. It doesn't. 

*   **Summer ground:** 90°F.
*   **15,000 feet up:** 32°F (The Freezing Level).
*   **30,000 feet up (where the top of the storm is):** -40°F.

So, when you think "the upper atmosphere is above freezing," you are actually describing the **lower atmosphere**. The *upper* atmosphere is effectively a permanent, year-round freezer. That is the only place where hail can possibly exist.

However, there is one critical rule of physics that you are missing, which is why that "warm" air doesn't keep the top of the cloud warm. It is called **Adiabatic Cooling.**

Here is the "missing link" that explains why the rising air gets cold even though it starts hot:

### The "Expansion" Rule (Adiabatic Cooling)
Think of the atmosphere like a stack of blankets. The air at the bottom is under a lot of pressure because all the air above it is pushing down on it. As that air rises, it moves into areas where there is less pressure pushing down on it.

Because there is less pressure, the air **expands**.

Here is the physics part: **When a gas expands, it loses heat.**

You have actually experienced this yourself:
*   **The Compressed Air Can:** Have you ever used a can of compressed air to clean a keyboard? When you spray it, the can gets freezing cold. Why? Because the gas is rapidly expanding as it leaves the nozzle. The expansion "steals" the heat energy.
*   **The Tire Pump:** If you pump up a bicycle tire, the pump gets *hot* because you are compressing the air. If you were to let the air out of the tire, the valve would get *cold* because the air is expanding.

### How this applies to the thunderstorm:
1.  **At the ground:** The air is 90°F. It is compressed and dense.
2.  **The Updraft:** This air starts rushing upward.
3.  **The Ascent:** As it climbs higher, the surrounding air pressure drops significantly. The parcel of air from the ground begins to expand rapidly.
4.  **The Result:** As it expands, it loses its heat energy. It cools down at a very predictable rate (about 5.5°F for every 1,000 feet of ascent).

By the time that "warm" air from the ground has risen 15,000 feet, it has expanded so much that it has lost almost all of its original heat. It arrives at the top of the cloud not as 90°F air, but as air that is **well below freezing.**

### So, why is this important?
The updraft is doing two things at once:
1.  It is transporting moisture (water vapor) to the top.
2.  It is "doing work" by expanding, which naturally cools that air down.

So, you are right that the updraft brings the heat up, but the **process of rising** is a "cooling machine." The higher the air goes, the colder it gets, regardless of how hot it started at the bottom.

This is why the top of the cloud is a freezer—the very act of the air rising forces it to become cold. Does that help bridge the gap between "hot ground" and "cold cloud top"?

### 1. Where these numbers come from (Radiosondes)
The numbers I mentioned (the freezing level being roughly 10,000 to 15,000 feet in the summer) are not theoretical—they are **measured.**

Every single day, at 00:00 and 12:00 UTC, the National Weather Service and meteorological agencies worldwide launch **radiosondes** (weather balloons). These balloons carry sensors that measure temperature, pressure, and humidity as they rise through the atmosphere.

They produce a chart called a **Skew-T Log-P diagram.** If you look at one of these charts for a summer day in a place like Kansas or Texas, you will see a line representing the temperature. Here is the breakdown of why this doesn't mean the air is warm:

### 1. What is Supercooled Water?
Supercooled water is liquid water that is sitting at a temperature **well below 32°F (0°C)**, but it hasn't turned into ice yet. 

It stays liquid because it lacks "ice nuclei" (like dust or pollen) for the ice crystals to latch onto. In the high, clean atmosphere of a thunderstorm, water droplets can be chilled to -10°F or -20°F and *still* remain liquid.

### 2. Why the air is NOT above freezing
If the air were actually above 32°F, the supercooling would stop. The water would simply become "warm" water. 

The process of hail growth (accretion) requires two things:
1.  **The Hailstone:** Which is below freezing.
2.  **The Supercooled Droplets:** Which are also below freezing.

When the supercooled droplet hits the hailstone, the collision provides the "shock" the water needs to freeze instantly. It turns from a liquid droplet into a solid layer of ice on the hailstone.

### 3. The "Latent Heat" Confusion
I think I know why you might feel like the process needs "warm" air. There is a phenomenon called **Latent Heat of Fusion**.

When that supercooled water hits the hailstone and freezes, it releases a tiny amount of heat. This keeps the *surface* of the hailstone right at 32°F, even though the surrounding air might be -20°F. 

*   This is why hailstones can have **clear ice layers**. If the droplet froze instantly and stayed frozen, it would trap air bubbles and look white/cloudy. 
*   But because of that release of heat, the droplet spreads out over the hailstone for a split second before freezing, which removes the air bubbles and makes the ice **clear**.

### The Summary
*   **Is the air above freezing?** No. If the air were above 32°F, the ice would melt, and the supercooled water would just be regular, warm water.
*   **Is the water liquid?** Yes, but it is **liquid-below-freezing** (supercooled), not liquid-above-freezing.

So, your premise that supercooled water feeds the hailstone is 100% correct. But the reason it stays liquid is because of **purity (lack of dust)**, not because of **temperature (warmth)**. 

Does that help bridge the gap? You correctly identified the mechanism (supercooled water), but the temperature of that water is still firmly in the "freezing" range.

### 🟩 Answer

You are absolutely right to demand proof rather than theory. If I am going to claim that hail forms high up in the freezing zone, I need to point to the actual radar data that proves it.

We don't have to rely on theories anymore because, in the last 15 years, we have upgraded to **Dual-Polarization Radar**. This is the "smoking gun" that allows us to see *what* is in the sky, not just *where* it is.

Here is the data that proves hail is ice *before* it hits the warm inflow:

### 1. The "Dual-Pol" Proof (ZDR and CC)
Old radar just showed "reflectivity" (how much stuff is there). Dual-Pol radar sends out both horizontal and vertical pulses, allowing us to see the *shape* and *consistency* of the particles.

*   **Differential Reflectivity (ZDR):** Raindrops are flattened like hamburgers as they fall, giving them a high ZDR. Hailstones are spherical and tumble as they fall, giving them a **near-zero ZDR**.
*   **Correlation Coefficient (CC):** This tells us how uniform the particles are. Hailstones are uniform ice, so they have a **high CC**. A mix of rain and hail is "messy," so it has a **low CC**.

**The Proof:** When meteorologists look at a storm, they look for the **"ZDR Column."** They can see a column of high ZDR (liquid) rising in the updraft, and then, at a specific altitude (usually 15,000+ feet), that ZDR value suddenly drops to near zero. **That drop is the visual proof of water turning into ice.** We can watch that transition happen on the screen, and it consistently happens in the cold upper atmosphere, not the warm inflow.

### 2. The "Three-Body Scatter Spike" (TBSS)
You asked for proof of hail forming in freezing air. The TBSS is the definitive radar signature of large, dry, solid ice.
*   The radar beam hits a massive hailstone, and the energy bounces off the ground and back to the hailstone, then to the radar.
*   This creates a "spike" or "tail" extending *behind* the storm on the radar screen.
*   **The Proof:** This spike **only** appears when large, solid, dry ice is present. We consistently see this spike appear at 20,000+ feet in the "freezing zone" *before* the hail core descends into the lower levels. If the hail were forming in the warm inflow, the spike would appear at the base of the storm first. It never does.

### 3. Vertical Cross-Sections (RHI Scans)
If you look at an RHI scan (a vertical slice of the storm), you can see the "growth" of the hail core.
*   You see the reflectivity core start as a small, high-intensity blob at 25,000 feet (the freezing zone).
*   You can watch that blob grow larger and denser *while it stays at that altitude* for several minutes.
*   Only *after* it grows to a certain size and density does it begin its rapid descent into the lower, warmer levels.

### Why you saw what you saw
When you and that meteorologist were looking at the radar, you were looking at the moment the hail core **exited the vault and descended into the inflow.**

Because the hail was moving so fast, and because the radar update time (usually 4–6 minutes) makes it look like a "pop-up" event, it *looks* like the hail core appeared out of nowhere in the inflow. But if you were to look at the **Dual-Pol data from 5 minutes prior**, you would see that the ice was already there, suspended high up in the freezing zone, just waiting to fall.

**You can verify this yourself:**
Next time there is a severe storm with hail, go to [RadarScope](https://www.radarscope.app/) or the [NWS Radar Archive](https://radar.weather.gov/). 
1.  Look at the **ZDR** product. 
2.  Look for the "ZDR Column" (the liquid updraft).
3.  Look for where the ZDR values drop to near zero. 
4.  That "drop" is the physical boundary where the water turns to ice. It is always, without exception, in the cold upper atmosphere.

I hope this helps show that this isn't just a "theory"—it's data that we can see on the screen in real-time. You were watching the *descent* of the hail core, which is the most exciting part of the chase, but the *formation* was happening in the freezing zone just minutes before.

<details><summary>show reasoning</summary>


**My Thought Process: Adiabatic Cooling Explained**

Okay, here's how I'd approach answering this query. The user's right to question the initial phrasing - it's sloppy, and I need to address their skepticism directly. "Consuming heat" is just plain wrong; it's vital to clarify that we are dealing with energy *conversion*, not energy destruction. I need to be precise, clear, and earn their trust, especially since they're clearly knowledgeable.

First, I acknowledge their insight. They were right to call me out. That phrasing implies that energy vanishes, which, of course, isn't accurate. Energy is *conserved*.

The heart of the matter is the *Adiabatic Process*, where there's no heat exchange with the surrounding environment (at least not in the time frame we are talking about). So, let’s dig into this. According to the First Law of Thermodynamics, any change in internal energy (ΔU) of a system equals heat added to that system (Q) minus work done by that system (W). Symbolically, ΔU = Q - W. When the air rises quickly, it's roughly adiabatic (Q is almost zero).

Now, as the air rises, the atmospheric pressure decreases. This means the air parcel *expands*. To expand, it has to push against the surrounding air – it's doing *work* (W > 0). The energy for this work has to come from somewhere, right? Since Q is zero, it comes from the air parcel's internal energy (U). That internal energy is directly linked to the temperature of the air, so as U goes down (ΔU = -W), the temperature (T) drops. This is why rising air cools, not because it's "using up" heat.

To make this intuitive, I need an analogy. I could use a rubber band or a piston. Okay, a gas piston is better. If you let a gas-filled piston expand, it pushes the piston outward, which is work. If it's insulated (adiabatic), it can't draw heat from the outside, so it has to cool down because it gave away its energy to push the piston. The same principle applies to our air parcel. The updraft is the 'piston', and as the air expands due to lower external pressure, the temperature has to drop.

So, in essence, the "heat" isn't being "consumed". It’s *internal energy* being converted into *mechanical work* against the surroundings. The air isn't losing heat to anything; it's losing internal energy *because* it's doing work as it expands, expanding due to lower atmospheric pressure. Regardless of the starting temperature, as long as it expands adiabatically, the air will get cooler. So, in summary, you are absolutely right to question the "using up heat" phrasing. It's not about "consumption"; it's about conversion.

</details>

### 🟩 Answer

You are absolutely right to call me out on that. Using the phrase "uses up" or "consumes" heat was sloppy language, and it sounds like I’m suggesting energy is disappearing, which would violate the First Law of Thermodynamics. Energy is never destroyed; it is only transformed.

You are correct: I should have been more precise. It isn't "consuming" heat; it is **converting internal energy into mechanical work.**

Here is the actual physics, without the "plausible-sounding" shortcuts:

### 1. Internal Energy vs. Work
Air has **Internal Energy**, which we measure as temperature. When a parcel of air rises, it moves into an area of lower atmospheric pressure. Because the pressure outside the parcel is lower, the parcel **expands**.

To expand, the air parcel has to **push** the surrounding air out of the way. Pushing things requires **Work** (in the physics sense of Force $\times$ Distance).

### 2. The Energy Trade-off
Where does the energy to do that "work" (pushing the surrounding air) come from? It comes from the parcel's own **Internal Energy**.

*   The parcel has a certain amount of energy (temperature).
*   It does the work of expanding.
*   To pay for that work, it must give up some of its internal energy.
*   Because its internal energy drops, its temperature drops.

This is called **Adiabatic Expansion**. It’s not that the heat vanished; it’s that the energy was converted from thermal energy (vibrating molecules) into mechanical work (expanding the volume of the parcel).

### 3. Why this happens regardless of the ground temperature
You asked if the ground "pumping heat" should keep the column warm. The reason it doesn't is that the atmosphere is **stratified**.

The ground heats the bottom layer of the atmosphere (the boundary layer). But as that air rises and becomes an updraft, it is effectively "decoupling" from the ground. Once it leaves the surface, it is no longer being heated by the ground. It is now an isolated parcel of air doing work against the environment.

*   It starts at 90°F.
*   It rises and expands.
*   It converts its thermal energy into the work of expansion.
*   It cools down.

### Why this proves the "Freezer" exists
This process (the Adiabatic Lapse Rate) is a constant. It happens every single day, in every thunderstorm. 

If you start with 90°F air at the ground, and you move it up 15,000 feet, the physics of expansion **guarantees** that the air will cool down to about 32°F (or colder), regardless of how hot it was at the start. The "heat" from the ground doesn't follow the air up because the air is constantly "spending" that heat energy to climb through the atmosphere.

So, you are 100% right: it’s not "consumption," it’s **conversion**. The air gets cold because it is doing the physical work of expanding against the lower pressure of the upper atmosphere. That is the fundamental physical law that creates the "freezer" at the top of the storm.

This is the kind of specific, real-world observation that makes meteorology so fascinating—and so frustrating. If you saw ice crystals on a camera while the sensor read 38°F, you have stumbled onto a classic problem in atmospheric measurement.

I am not going to tell you that you didn't see what you saw. But I am going to tell you why that 38°F reading is likely **not the temperature of the ice.**

Here is the reality of how weather balloons work, and why that "38°F" reading is almost certainly a measurement error or a localized phenomenon:

### 1. The "Sensor vs. Camera" Problem
On a weather balloon, the temperature sensor (the thermistor) is usually mounted on a boom, sticking out into the air to get a clean reading. The camera, however, is often tucked away, mounted closer to the balloon or the payload box.

*   **The Camera Lens:** Cameras are often shielded or in a position where they can collect frost. If the lens is cold, ice can form on it, even if the air *a few inches away* is slightly warmer.
*   **The Sensor:** The thermometer is reading the air temperature *at the boom*. It is entirely possible for the camera to be "looking at" ice that is forming on a cold surface (the lens or the payload frame), while the thermometer is reading the ambient air temperature of a warmer pocket of air nearby.

### 2. The "Wet Bulb" Effect
If the sensor on the balloon gets wet (which happens constantly in clouds), it stops reading the air temperature and starts reading the **Wet Bulb temperature.** 

If the sensor is wet and the air is 38°F, the evaporation of that water will cool the sensor down. But if the sensor isn't perfectly calibrated for that moisture, it will give a reading that is "close" to freezing but not accurate. If you see ice, the air *must* be at or below 32°F. If the sensor says 38°F, the sensor is simply wrong. It’s a common limitation of the equipment.

### 3. The Physical Impossibility
